#10996. Scientists discover AI can make humans more creative

Artificial intelligence (AI) is commonly viewed as a technology designed to automate work and potentially replace human labor. However, new research from Swansea University offers a different perspective. The findings suggest that AI can also function as a creative collaborator that encourages exploration, engagement, and inspiration.

Researchers from the University's Computer Science Department carried out one of the largest studies so far examining how people work alongside AI during creative design tasks. More than 800 participants joined an online experiment where they used an AI-supported system to design virtual cars.

Rather than quietly optimizing designs behind the scenes, the system used a method called MAP-Elites to produce visual galleries filled with many different design possibilities. These galleries showed a wide spectrum of car concepts, including highly effective designs, unusual ideas, and even some intentionally flawed options.

Turing Fellow Dr. Sean Walton, Associate Professor of Computer Science and the study's lead author, explained: "People often think of AI as something that speeds up tasks or improves efficiency, but our findings suggest something far more interesting. When people were shown AI-generated design suggestions, they spent more time on the task, produced better designs and felt more involved. It was not just about efficiency. It was about creativity and collaboration."

Why Traditional AI Evaluation May Be Too Limited

The study, published in the ACM journal Transactions on Interactive Intelligent Systems, also highlights a problem with how AI design tools are typically assessed. Standard metrics often focus on simple behaviors, such as how frequently users click on or copy AI suggestions. According to the researchers, these measures overlook important aspects of the experience, including how the technology influences people's thoughts, emotions, and willingness to explore new ideas.

The Swansea researchers argue that AI systems should be evaluated using broader methods that capture these deeper effects. Understanding how AI shapes human thinking and engagement could provide a more complete picture of its impact.

Dr. Walton emphasized that variety in AI-generated output played a crucial role in the experiment. "Our study highlights the importance of diversity in AI output. Participants responded most positively to galleries that included a wide variety of ideas, including bad ones! These helped them move beyond their initial assumptions and explore a broader design space. This structured diversity prevented early fixation and encouraged creative risk-taking.

"As AI becomes increasingly embedded in creative fields, from engineering and architecture to music and game design, understanding how humans and intelligent systems work together is essential. As the technology evolves, the question is not only what AI can do but how it can help us think, create and collaborate more effectively."

Materials provided by Swansea University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10990. Former senator whose arrest went viral on YouTube wins another election

A former senator in Minnesota has been elected to a city council a year after a conviction on two felony burglary counts and being highlighted by a true crime YouTuber.

Back in August 2025, the true-crime YouTube channel Midwest Safety highlighted the story of Nicole Mitchell, the former Minnesota state senator.

Mitchell had been arrested by police back in 2024 after gaining entry to her stepmother’s house. The politician stated that she was there to retrieve belongings owned by her recently deceased dad because her stepmother “wouldn’t talk to me anymore.”

However, she then changed her story during a court trial, saying she wanted to check on the well-being of her stepmother. Mitchell was found guilty after a three-hour hearing and sentenced to 180 days in county jail. She was released in January 2026 after an early release appeal.

Mitchell initially said that she wouldn’t resign from her post, but ultimately did. Yet, she has now gotten back into politics.

The former state senator has been elected to a position on the Woodbury City Council, which has come under some scrutiny given her previous conviction.

As per local reports, the Minnesota Department of Corrections website previously stated that “if you are convicted of a felony you lose the right to hold public office until court-ordered discharge from probation or the end of your sentence.”

Some noted that was changed in 2023, but others have argued that the change only surrodned the ability to vote for a candidate, not actually run for office.

#10988. AI-powered robot learns how to harvest tomatoes more efficiently

Farm labor shortages are pushing agriculture toward greater automation, especially when it comes to harvesting. But not all crops are easy for machines to handle. Tomatoes, for example, grow in clusters, which means a robot must carefully select ripe fruit while leaving unripe ones untouched. This requires precise control and smart decision-making.

To tackle this challenge, Assistant Professor Takuya Fujinaga of Osaka Metropolitan University's Graduate School of Engineering developed a system that trains robots to assess how easy each tomato is to harvest before attempting to pick it.

His approach combines image recognition with statistical analysis to determine the best angle for picking each fruit. The robot analyzes visual details such as the tomato itself, its stems, and whether it is hidden behind leaves or other parts of the plant. These inputs guide the robot in choosing the most effective way to approach and pick the fruit.

From Detection to "Harvest-Ease" Decision-Making

This method shifts away from traditional systems that focus only on detecting and identifying fruit. Instead, Fujinaga introduces what he calls "harvest-ease estimation." "This moves beyond simply asking 'can a robot pick a tomato?' to thinking about 'how likely is a successful pick?', which is more meaningful for real-world farming," he explained.

In testing, the system achieved an 81% success rate, exceeding expectations. About one-quarter of the successful picks came from tomatoes that were harvested from the side after an initial front-facing attempt failed. This indicates the robot can adjust its approach when the first attempt is not successful.

The research underscores how many variables affect robotic harvesting, including how tomatoes cluster, the shape and position of stems, surrounding leaves, and visual obstruction. "This research establishes 'ease of harvesting' as a quantitatively evaluable metric, bringing us one step closer to the realization of agricultural robots that can make informed decisions and act intelligently," Fujinaga said.

Future of Human-Robot Collaboration in Farming

Looking ahead, Fujinaga envisions robots that can independently judge when crops are ready to be picked. "This is expected to usher in a new form of agriculture where robots and humans collaborate," he explained. "Robots will automatically harvest tomatoes that are easy to pick, while humans will handle the more challenging fruits."

The findings were published in Smart Agricultural Technology.

#10986. Toyota is bringing a kei truck to America that only goes 25mph

Toyota is bringing a tiny kei truck to America in 2027 that’s only 11 feet long and has a top speed of 25mph.

Whether you’ve traveled to Japan or played Forza Horizon 6, you’ll likely have seen your fair share of kei trucks. These little vehicles are known for their small size and ability to squeeze into tight spaces with very little problem. They’re both practical for those with minimal space and, well, they’re also rather adorable.

Now, Toyota is bringing this pint-sized approach to the US with a new kei truck model that will be released in the US next year.

Announced on November 10, the KEI-01 measures just over 11 feet long and less than five feet wide. The miniature pickup also comes with a 660cc three-cylinder engine, selectable four-wheel drive, an enclosed cabin, and a cargo bed with three folding sides.

Despite its small size, the truck can carry up to 600 pounds and is capable of towing small trailers/equpment. Inside, drivers get heating, air conditioning, sliding and reclining seats, and storage behind the seats for tools and other gear. Oh, and it also retains its signature right-hand-drive layout.

There is just one catch, though: the KEI-01 tops out at just 25mph. It’s not exactly the fastest truck on the market, but Toyota says the little truck has mainly been designed for use on farms, university campuses, and businesses.

If you’re hoping to buy your own tiny kei truck, you’ll have to wait, as Toyota will initially only sell it to businesses and organizations through selected dealers. However, the Japanese car company has said it may open sales to everyone else later if there’s enough demand.

#10984. DNA robots could deliver drugs and hunt viruses inside your body

Imagine tiny robots made from DNA moving through the bloodstream, delivering drugs exactly where they are needed and targeting threats such as cancer cells or viruses. These microscopic machines could also assemble ultra-precise data storage systems and computing devices at the nanometer scale. Although the possibilities are remarkable, most DNA robots today remain in early experimental stages and are better understood as proof of concept rather than practical tools.

Researchers are exploring how DNA can be engineered into working machines using creative design approaches. These include building rigid DNA joints, incorporating flexible components, and using folding techniques inspired by origami. By applying principles from larger-scale robotics such as rigid, compliant and origami robots, scientists are adapting familiar mechanical concepts to the nanoscale. This allows DNA-based systems to carry out controlled and repeatable tasks despite their extremely small size.

Guiding the motion of DNA robots in a constantly shifting molecular environment is a major challenge. To address this, scientists have developed control systems that help these machines behave in predictable ways. One important method involves DNA strand displacement, a biochemical process that enables precise programming of movement using specific DNA sequences labeled as "fuel" and "structure."

In addition to biochemical control, external physical signals such as electric fields, magnetic fields, and light can direct how these robots move. Together, these approaches provide a toolkit for fine-tuning the behavior of DNA machines with a high degree of accuracy.

The potential uses for DNA robots extend well beyond laboratory experiments. In medicine, they could function as "nano-surgeons," locating diseased cells and delivering targeted treatments with precision. Researchers are also exploring whether these machines could capture viruses like SARS-CoV-2, with future systems potentially operating as fully autonomous drug delivery platforms.

DNA robots may also play a role in advanced manufacturing. Acting as programmable templates, they could position nanoparticles with sub-nanometer accuracy. This capability could lead to breakthroughs in molecular computing and highly efficient optical devices that outperform current technologies.

Despite rapid progress, several obstacles remain. Moving from large-scale systems to molecular machines introduces challenges such as Brownian motion, which makes precise control more difficult. Many current DNA robot designs are still relatively simple and operate in isolation, limiting their usefulness in complex real-world environments.

There are also gaps in foundational knowledge. Researchers still lack detailed databases describing the mechanical properties of DNA structures, and simulation tools for predicting behavior at this scale are not yet fully developed.

To overcome these barriers, scientists emphasize the need for collaboration across disciplines. Proposed solutions include creating standardized DNA "parts libraries," using artificial intelligence to improve design and simulation, and advancing bio-manufacturing methods. Progress in these areas will be essential for scaling DNA robots and integrating them into practical applications in healthcare, manufacturing, and beyond.

"The robots of tomorrow won't just be made of metal and plastic," says the research team. "They will be biological, programmable, and intelligent. They will be the tools that allow us to finally master the molecular world."

This review took place in early September, with requests being made to Rockstar so that officials could see parts of the game early and then vote again.

#10976. How to Optimize AI-Generated Images for Faster Websites

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI-generated images are becoming a common part of modern web development. Developers use them for website banners, product illustrations, landing pages, blog posts, and digital interfaces. However, generating an image is only one part of the process. Before publishing it, developers need to ensure that the image loads quickly, displays correctly across devices, and maintains its visual quality.

Large image files can increase page size, consume unnecessary bandwidth, and delay the appearance of important content. These problems can affect user experience and website performance, particularly on mobile devices and slower internet connections.

A reliable image optimization workflow helps address these challenges. By selecting appropriate dimensions, choosing efficient image formats, automating image processing, and implementing responsive delivery, developers can improve loading performance without sacrificing visual quality.

This guide explains how to optimize AI-generated images using practical techniques and development tools.

Images often account for a significant portion of a webpage's total download size. Uploading large, unoptimized images can make pages slower and increase the amount of data visitors need to download.

Several common problems contribute to poor image performance:

Incorrect dimensions waste bandwidth on smaller screens.

Missing image dimensions can cause unexpected layout shifts.

Poor responsive configuration can make browsers download unnecessarily large images.

Incorrect loading priorities can delay important visual content.

These issues can affect Core Web Vitals, including Largest Contentful Paint (LCP), which measures how quickly the largest visible content element appears.

For developers, image optimization should therefore be part of the website development workflow rather than a final task performed immediately before publication.

Before optimizing an image, determine where it will appear on the website. A full-width banner requires different dimensions from a product thumbnail or a small interface graphic.

For example, a website might use the following dimensions:

These dimensions are examples rather than fixed requirements. The appropriate size depends on the website layout, responsive breakpoints, and supported devices.

When preparing source visuals, developers can explore different AI image generation options and consider how the generated images will fit their intended layouts.

Once the image is ready, keep a high-quality master file. This allows developers to create smaller versions for different screen sizes without repeatedly compressing an already optimized image.

Avoid enlarging small images unnecessarily because upscaling can introduce blur and visible artifacts.

Choosing the right format can reduce image size while preserving acceptable visual quality.

Three commonly used formats are AVIF, WebP, and JPEG.

AVIF: Often provides efficient compression for photographs and detailed illustrations. It can significantly reduce file sizes, although encoding may take longer.

WebP: Supports lossy and lossless compression, transparency, and animation. It offers broad browser support and is suitable for many website images.

JPEG: Remains useful for photographs and situations where compatibility with older software or systems is important.

PNG is appropriate for certain lossless graphics, screenshots, and images that require transparency. However, it can produce unnecessarily large files when used for photographic content.

No format is automatically the best choice for every image. Developers should compare file sizes and inspect visual quality before selecting the final output.

A practical approach is to generate AVIF and WebP versions and provide a JPEG fallback where necessary.

Manually resizing and converting images becomes time-consuming when a website contains dozens or hundreds of visual assets.

Automation makes the process more consistent. Developers can use Sharp, a Node.js image-processing library, to resize images, convert formats, and generate multiple versions from a single source file.

Next, create a file named build-images.mjs:

The script creates multiple image sizes in both WebP and AVIF formats.

The quality settings are starting points, not universal recommendations. Developers should adjust them after checking the resulting file sizes and visual details.

This approach is particularly useful for content-heavy websites, product catalogs, and applications that publish images regularly.

Responsive image delivery allows browsers to select an appropriate image based on the visitor's screen and the space available in the layout.

HTML provides two important attributes for this purpose: srcset and sizes.

The srcset attribute lists the available image files and their widths. The sizes attribute describes how wide the image is expected to appear at different viewport sizes.

In this example, the image is expected to occupy approximately 720 pixels on wider screens and nearly the full viewport width on smaller screens.

The browser uses this information to choose an appropriate image candidate.

One common mistake is setting sizes="100vw" for an image that occupies only a small part of the page. This can encourage the browser to download a larger file than necessary.

Always ensure that the sizes attribute reflects the actual website layout.

The HTML picture element allows developers to provide multiple image formats and a fallback option.

The browser selects a supported format, while the img element provides the fallback image and alternative text.

Developers should verify that every referenced file exists and that its dimensions match the intended layout.

The picture element can also support different crops for desktop and mobile devices. This is useful when a wide banner needs a separate composition for smaller screens.

Images without defined dimensions can cause content to move while the browser loads the file. This behavior contributes to Cumulative Layout Shift (CLS).

Including width and height attributes helps browsers reserve the appropriate space before the image finishes loading.

For responsive layouts, developers can use CSS:

This allows images to scale within their containers while maintaining their proportions.

Images below the initial viewport can generally use native lazy loading:

However, the main hero image should not normally be lazy-loaded when it is responsible for LCP. Delaying an important above-the-fold image can make the page appear slower.

Where appropriate, developers can use fetchpriority="high" for the main image to help the browser prioritize its download.

The correct strategy depends on the image's position and importance within the page.

Image optimization involves more than file size and dimensions. Developers should also consider accessibility, file organization, and metadata.

Alternative text helps people using screen readers understand meaningful images. It should describe the image's purpose in the context of the page.

Decorative images that provide no useful information should generally use an empty alt attribute.

File names should also be descriptive. Names such as home-office-sunrise.webp are easier to understand and maintain than generic names such as image-final-v3.png.

Metadata requires additional consideration. Image-processing tools may remove metadata by default to reduce file size. This can include information that a project needs to preserve, depending on its requirements.

Before removing metadata, developers should review whether the website or client requires attribution, provenance information, or other embedded details.

After implementing an optimization workflow, test the actual website rather than relying only on exported file sizes.

Chrome DevTools provides useful information about image requests.

Open Developer Tools and select the Network panel.

Resize the viewport or use device emulation.

Check whether the browser downloads appropriately sized images.

Developers can also inspect the image selected by the browser through the console:

This returns the current image URL selected for that element.

For websites with multiple images, inspect the relevant elements individually.

Finally, run an audit using Lighthouse or PageSpeed Insights. These tools can identify issues involving oversized images, image formats, loading priorities, and other performance factors.

Image optimization is only one part of website performance. Server response times, caching, CSS, JavaScript, and third-party scripts can also influence loading speed.

Several mistakes can reduce the effectiveness of an image optimization workflow:

Uploading original images without checking their dimensions.

Assuming one format always produces the smallest file.

Omitting the sizes attribute from responsive images.

Lazy-loading important above-the-fold images.

Applying excessive compression and damaging visual details.

Removing metadata without checking project requirements.

Measuring file size without inspecting the final visual quality.

A consistent testing process helps identify these issues before they affect visitors.

No. AVIF often provides efficient compression, but the results depend on the image, encoding settings, and quality requirements. Comparing both formats is the best way to choose an appropriate option.

Four to six sizes may be sufficient for many websites. The ideal number depends on the layout, responsive breakpoints, and supported devices.

Yes. Reducing the size of the main content image, providing responsive versions, and avoiding unnecessary loading delays can improve LCP when that image is the largest contentful element.

No. Lazy loading is useful for images outside the initial viewport. Important images near the top of the page should generally load promptly.

The fundamental techniques are the same as those used for conventional images. However, AI-generated visuals may contain complex textures, gradients, and fine details that require careful inspection after compression.

AI-generated images can improve website design and make it easier to create custom visual content. However, efficient delivery requires more than exporting an image and uploading it to a website.

Developers should choose suitable dimensions, compare modern image formats, automate processing, and implement responsive image delivery. Including image dimensions helps prevent layout shifts, while appropriate loading priorities can improve the experience for visitors.

By testing images across different devices and reviewing their actual performance, development teams can maintain visual quality while reducing unnecessary downloads.

A repeatable optimization workflow makes it easier to publish new visuals without sacrificing website speed, accessibility, or reliability.

#10975. Ethics and Society Newsletter #6: Building Better AI: The Importance of Data Quality

#10974. AI breakthrough cuts energy use by 100x while boosting accuracy

Artificial intelligence is consuming enormous amounts of electricity in the United States. According to the International Energy Agency, AI systems and data centers used about 415 terawatt hours of power in 2024. That accounts for more than 10% of the country's total electricity production, and demand is projected to double by 2030.

This rapid growth has raised concerns about sustainability. In response, researchers at a School of Engineering have created a proof-of-concept AI system designed to be far more efficient. Their approach could reduce energy use by up to 100 times while also improving performance on tasks.

A Hybrid Approach Called Neuro-Symbolic AI

The research comes from the laboratory of Matthias Scheutz, Karol Family Applied Technology Professor. His team is developing neuro-symbolic AI, which combines traditional neural networks with symbolic reasoning. This method mirrors how people approach problems by breaking them into steps and categories.

The work will be presented at the International Conference of Robotics and Automation in Vienna in May and will appear in the conference proceedings.

Teaching Robots to See, Understand, and Act

Unlike familiar large language models (LLMs) such as ChatGPT and Gemini, the team focuses on AI systems used in robotics. These systems are known as visual-language-action (VLA) models. They extend LLM capabilities by incorporating vision and physical movement.

VLA models take in visual data from cameras and instructions from language, then translate that information into real-world actions. For example, they can control a robot's wheels, arms, or fingers to complete a task.

Why Traditional AI Struggles With Simple Tasks

Conventional VLA systems rely heavily on data and trial-and-error learning. If a robot is asked to stack blocks into a tower, it must first analyze the scene, identify each block, and determine how to place them correctly.

This process often leads to mistakes. Shadows may confuse the system about a block's shape, or the robot may place pieces incorrectly, causing the structure to collapse.

These errors are similar to the problems seen in LLMs. Just as robots can misplace blocks, chatbots can generate false or misleading outputs. Examples include fabricating legal cases or producing images with unrealistic details such as extra fingers.

How Symbolic Reasoning Improves Accuracy and Efficiency

Symbolic reasoning offers a different strategy. Instead of relying only on patterns from data, it uses rules and abstract concepts such as shape and balance. This allows the system to plan more effectively and avoid unnecessary trial and error.

"Like an LLM, VLA models act on statistical results from large training sets of similar scenarios, but that can lead to errors," said Scheutz. "A neuro-symbolic VLA can apply rules that limit the amount of trial and error during learning and get to a solution much faster. Not only does it complete the task much faster, but the time spent on training the system is significantly reduced."

The researchers tested their system using the Tower of Hanoi puzzle, a classic problem that requires careful planning.

The neuro-symbolic VLA achieved a 95% success rate, compared with just 34% for standard systems. When given a more complex version of the puzzle that it had not encountered before, the hybrid system still succeeded 78% of the time. Traditional models failed every attempt.

Training time also dropped sharply. The new system learned the task in only 34 minutes, while conventional models required more than a day and a half.

Massive Energy Savings in Training and Use

Energy consumption was reduced dramatically as well. Training the neuro-symbolic model required only 1% of the energy used by a standard VLA system. During operation, it used just 5% of the energy needed by conventional approaches.

Scheutz compared this inefficiency to everyday AI tools. "These systems are just trying to predict the next word or action in a sequence, but that can be imperfect, and they can come up with inaccurate results or hallucinations. Their energy expense is often disproportionate to the task. For example, when you search on Google, the AI summary at the top of the page consumes up to 100 times more energy than the generation of the website listings."

The Growing Strain of AI on Power Infrastructure

As AI adoption accelerates across industries, demand for computing power continues to climb. Companies are building increasingly large data centers, some of which require hundreds of megawatts of electricity. That level of consumption can exceed the needs of entire small cities.

This trend has sparked a race to expand infrastructure, raising concerns about long-term energy limits.

The researchers suggest that current approaches based on LLMs and VLAs may not be sustainable in the long run. While these systems are powerful, they consume large amounts of energy and can still produce unreliable results.

In contrast, neuro-symbolic AI offers a different direction. By combining learning with structured reasoning, it may provide a more efficient and dependable foundation for future AI systems.

Materials provided by Tufts University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10972. Microsoft now lets you turn off AI search results completely in Bing

Microsoft is giving Bing users the option to turn off AI-generated answers in their search results, allowing them to browse the internet without being shown AI summaries.

AI has become a major part of online searches in recent years, with companies like Microsoft and Google using the technology to generate quick answers to questions instead of simply providing links to websites.

While this can be useful for quickly finding information, not everyone wants AI to be involved in their searches at all times.

Now, Microsoft is giving people more control over how they use Bing, with two simple ways to remove AI-generated answers from their results.

In a November 11 announcement, Microsoft revealed a new browser extension called Bing AI Search Choice, which lets users switch AI-generated answers on or off.

Once installed, the extension adds a toggle that can be used to choose between traditional search results and AI-powered summaries.

The setting applies to all searches made through Bing, meaning users won’t have to change their preferences every time they look something up.

However, the extension is currently only available for Google Chrome and Microsoft Edge, and installing it will also make Bing your default search engine.

For those who don’t want to install anything, simply adding “-ai” to the end of a Bing search will remove AI-generated answers from the results. For example, searching for “best games of 2026 -ai” will bring up results without the usual AI summaries.

Microsoft explained that the feature is about giving people the freedom to decide how they want to search.

Bing AI is now available to everyone and it just got smarter

“AI is doing powerful things for search, and our research shows users want to have the option to choose whether or not AI is involved in every session,” the company said.

“Sometimes a quick AI-generated summary is exactly what you need. Other times, you’d rather scan the results, compare sources and decide for yourself what information to trust.”

The browser extension is currently in preview, meaning Microsoft could make further changes based on user feedback.

#10966. This new chip survives 1300°F (700°C) and could change AI forever

Modern electronics power everything from smartphones to satellites, but they all share a major limitation. Heat. Once temperatures climb above roughly 200 degrees Celsius, most devices begin to break down. For decades, this thermal barrier has been one of the toughest challenges in engineering.

Researchers at the University of Southern California now believe they have found a way past that limit.

In a study published on March 26, 2026 in Science, a team led by Joshua Yang, Arthur B. Freeman Chair Professor at the Ming Hsieh Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering at the USC Viterbi School of Engineering and the USC School of Advanced Computing, unveiled a new type of memory device that continues to operate at 700 degrees Celsius (~1300 degrees Fahrenheit). That temperature exceeds molten lava and goes far beyond anything previously achieved for this class of technology. The device showed no sign of failure. In fact, 700 degrees was simply the maximum their equipment could test.

"You may call it a revolution," Yang said. "It is the best high-temperature memory ever demonstrated."

The new device is known as a memristor, a nanoscale component that can both store data and perform computations. It is constructed like a microscopic layered structure, with two electrodes on either side and a thin ceramic layer in between.

Jian Zhao, the study's first author, built the device using tungsten for the top electrode, hafnium oxide ceramic in the middle, and graphene for the bottom layer. Tungsten has the highest melting point of any element, while graphene, a single-atom-thick sheet of carbon, is known for its exceptional strength and heat resistance.

This combination produced remarkable performance. The device retained data for more than 50 hours at 700 degrees without needing to be refreshed. It also endured over one billion switching cycles at that temperature and operated at just 1.5 volts with speeds measured in tens of nanoseconds.

The discovery was not part of the team's original plan. They were initially attempting to create a different graphene-based device, which did not work as intended. Along the way, they encountered something surprising.

"To be honest, it was by accident, as most discoveries are," Yang said. "If you can predict it, it's usually not surprising, and probably not significant enough."

Further investigation revealed why the device performed so well. In conventional electronics, heat causes metal atoms in the top electrode to slowly migrate through the ceramic layer. Eventually, they reach the bottom electrode, creating a permanent connection that short-circuits the device and leaves it stuck in the on state.

Graphene prevents this failure. Its interaction with tungsten is, as Yang described it, similar to oil and water. Tungsten atoms that approach the graphene surface cannot attach to it. Without a stable point to settle, they drift away instead of forming a conductive bridge. This prevents short circuits and preserves the device's function even under extreme heat.

The researchers confirmed this mechanism using advanced electron microscopy, spectroscopy, and quantum-level simulations. By understanding what happens at the atomic interface, they have turned an unexpected result into a principle that could guide future designs. Other materials with similar surface properties could be identified, which may help scale the technology for industrial production.

Electronics capable of operating above 500 degrees Celsius have long been a goal for space exploration. Venus, for example, has a surface temperature around that level, and every lander sent there has failed in part due to extreme heat. Current silicon-based chips cannot survive such conditions.

"We are now above 700 degrees, and we suspect it will go higher," Yang said.

The potential applications go far beyond space missions. Geothermal energy systems require electronics that can function deep underground, where surrounding rock can glow red-hot. Nuclear and fusion systems also expose equipment to intense heat. Even in everyday settings, durability improves significantly. A device rated for 700 degrees would be extremely robust at the roughly 125-degree temperatures often reached inside automotive electronics.

Why It Matters for Artificial Intelligence

In addition to storing data, the device offers a major advantage for artificial intelligence. Many AI systems rely heavily on matrix multiplication, a mathematical operation used in tasks like image recognition and language processing. Traditional computers perform these calculations step by step, consuming large amounts of energy.

Memristors approach the problem differently. By using Ohm's Law, where voltage times conductance equals current, the device performs calculations directly as electricity flows through it. The result is obtained instantly as the measured current.

"Over 92 percent of the computing in AI systems like ChatGPT is nothing but matrix multiplication," Yang said. "This type of device can perform that in the most efficient way, orders of magnitude faster and at lower energy."

Yang and three co-authors of the study (Qiangfei Xia, Miao Hu, and Ning Ge) have already co-founded a company called TetraMem to commercialize memristor-based AI chips at room temperature. Their lab is already using working chips from TetraMem for machine learning tasks. The high-temperature version described in this research could extend those capabilities to environments where traditional electronics cannot operate, allowing devices such as spacecraft or industrial sensors to process data directly on site.

From Lab Prototype to Real-World Technology

Despite the promising results, Yang emphasizes that practical applications are still some distance away. Memory is only one part of a complete computing system. High-temperature logic circuits will also need to be developed and integrated. In addition, the current devices were built manually at very small scales in a laboratory setting, so manufacturing at scale will take time.

"This is the first step," Yang said. "It's still a long way to go. But logically, you can see: now it makes it possible. The missing component has been made."

From a manufacturing perspective, two of the materials used in the device, tungsten and hafnium oxide, are already widely used in semiconductor production. Graphene is newer but is actively being developed by major companies such as TSMC and Samsung, and it has already been produced at wafer scale in research environments.

The work was conducted through the CONCRETE Center, short for Center of Neuromorphic Computing under Extreme Environments, a multi-university Center of Excellence led by USC and supported by the Air Force Office of Scientific Research and the Air Force Research Laboratory. Key experimental work was carried out in collaboration with Dr. Sabyasachi Ganguli's team at the AFRL Materials Lab in Dayton, Ohio, while theoretical analysis involved USC researchers and collaborators at Kumamoto University in Japan.

For Yang, publication in Science reflects more than a single achievement.

"Space exploration has never been so real, so close, and at such a large scale," he said. "This paper represents a critical leap into a much larger, more exciting frontier."

Materials provided by University of Southern California. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10963. How to Test AI-Generated Content Before It Reaches Your Users

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most teams now ship something an AI helped write: help-center articles, product descriptions, release notes, even code comments. The draft appears in seconds, and that speed is the trap. Nobody tests it the way they would test a new feature, so mistakes slip through that a quick review would have caught.

This guide is about the other side of AI testing. Instead of using AI to test your software, you test what AI hands you. Here is a simple routine that fits into a normal publishing or release process.

AI writing sounds confident whether or not it is right. A model can invent a statistic (researchers call this hallucination and have documented it across many text-generation systems), quote a feature your product does not have, or describe a setting that was renamed two releases ago. The sentence reads smoothly, so the error hides in plain sight.

Traditional QA does not cover this. A unit test tells you the page loads. It says nothing about whether the claim on the page is true. That gap is where most AI-related incidents begin.

1. Define what "good" means before you generate anything. Write down the audience, the claims you are willing to make and the sources you trust. A short brief makes the output easier to judge, because you are comparing it with something concrete.

2. Check every factual claim. Highlight each number, name and statement of fact, then verify it against a primary source. Google's own guidance on generative AI content tells publishers to fact-check AI output, including titles, descriptions and alt text, before publishing. If you cannot confirm a claim in a minute or two, cut it or rewrite it as something you can support.

3. Test the instructions. For tutorials and how-to content, follow the steps exactly as written on a clean machine or account. Commands that fail and menus that no longer exist show up immediately this way.

4. Review the voice. Replace vague phrases with specifics from your own experience: a real customer question, a real error message, a real number from your analytics. This is also what separates useful content from filler.

5. Keep a human owner. Every piece should have a named person who approved it. The NIST AI Risk Management Framework makes the same point at organization level: people stay accountable for how AI is used and evaluated. If something goes wrong later, you know who to ask and where to fix it.

Some teams also run drafts through an AI detector. That can be useful as a prompt for a closer look, for example when freelancers or vendors deliver work and you want to know how much of it was machine-generated. A high score is a reason to read more carefully. It is not proof of anything, and it should never decide an outcome on its own, because detectors make mistakes in both directions. A Stanford study found that GPT detectors are biased against non-native English writers, and short texts are easy to misjudge too. If you want a quick second opinion on a draft, a tool like Tester AI can give you a starting point for that conversation.

Review works best when it is not optional. A few small habits make it stick.

Treating the first draft as finished. It is a starting point. Plan time for review the same way you plan time for writing.

Reviewing for grammar only. Clean sentences are not evidence of correct content. Check meaning first and polish second.

Letting the tool grade its own work. Asking the same model to confirm its answer rarely catches real errors. Use a source or a person.

Skipping legal and brand review. Generated text can echo existing material or make promises your company cannot keep. Anything customer-facing deserves a second pair of eyes.

AI can save hours of drafting, but speed only helps if the result is trustworthy. Treat generated content like code from a new contributor: useful, often good, and always reviewed before it goes live. A short routine of defining quality, verifying facts, testing instructions and keeping a human owner will catch most problems before your users do.

Do I need to test AI-generated content if a human edits it? Yes. Editing for style does not confirm that the facts are correct. Verify claims separately from polishing the wording.

How long should a review take? For a typical article, expect roughly the time it takes to read it twice plus a few minutes per factual claim. Reviews are faster when the brief and sources are prepared in advance.

Can an AI detector prove that text was written by a machine? No. Detectors estimate likelihood, and they can be wrong. Use the result as a signal for human review, never as the final decision.

What should I check first? Start with numbers, names, dates and anything a customer could act on, such as prices, steps or legal statements. Those carry the highest risk if they are wrong.

#10960. This new chip could slash data center energy waste

As data centers consume more energy to support growing digital demands, engineers at the University of California San Diego have introduced a new chip design that could make powering graphics processing units (GPUs) more efficient. The innovation focuses on a key function in electronics: converting high voltages into the lower levels required by computing hardware. In laboratory testing, a prototype chip successfully performed this type of voltage conversion with high efficiency under conditions similar to those found in modern data centers.

The findings, published in Nature Communications, suggest the potential for smaller and more energy-efficient systems in advanced computing environments.

Rethinking DC-DC Converters for Modern Electronics

At the center of the new design is an improved version of a widely used component known as a DC-DC step-down converter. These converters are found in nearly all electronic devices and serve as a critical link between power sources and sensitive circuits. Their job is to take a high incoming voltage and reduce it to the exact level needed for safe operation.

In data centers, electricity is often distributed at 48 volts, while GPU processors typically require much lower voltages, usually between 1 and 5 volts. Efficiently managing this large voltage drop has become increasingly challenging as computing systems grow more powerful and compact.

Limits of Traditional Power Conversion Technology

Conventional step-down converters often struggle when dealing with large differences between input and output voltage. As that gap increases, efficiency drops and it becomes harder to supply enough current. Most existing designs rely on magnetic components such as inductors. While these components have been refined over many years, they are approaching their practical limits and are becoming harder to improve further.

"We've gotten so good at designing inductive converters that there's not really much room left to improve them to meet future needs," said study senior author Patrick Mercier, professor in the Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering at the UC San Diego Jacobs School of Engineering.

Exploring Piezoelectric Resonators as an Alternative

To move beyond these limitations, Mercier and his team, including first author Jae-Young Ko, an electrical and computer engineering Ph.D. student at UC San Diego, investigated a different approach using piezoelectric resonators. These small devices store and transfer energy through mechanical vibrations rather than magnetic fields.

Converters based on piezoelectric components could offer several advantages. They have the potential to be smaller, more energy dense, more efficient, and easier to manufacture at scale. "They have a lot of room to grow and have the potential to deliver better performance than anything that's come before them," Mercier said.

However, earlier versions of piezoelectric converters have had difficulty maintaining efficiency and delivering sufficient power when handling large voltage differences.

Hybrid Design Achieves High Efficiency and Power Output

To overcome these issues, the researchers created a hybrid converter that combines a piezoelectric resonator with small, commercially available capacitors arranged in a carefully designed configuration. This setup enables the system to handle larger voltage conversions more effectively.

The team incorporated this design into a prototype chip and tested its performance. The device successfully converted 48 volts down to 4.8 volts -- a level commonly required in data centers -- with a peak efficiency of 96.2 percent. It also delivered roughly four times more output current than previous piezoelectric-based designs.

This hybrid approach offers several benefits. It creates multiple pathways for energy to move through the system, reduces wasted power, and lessens the strain on the resonator. Together, these improvements enhance both efficiency and power delivery while only slightly increasing the chip's size.

Challenges and Next Steps for Real-World Use

Although the technology shows strong promise, it is still in the early stages of development. The researchers view it as an important step toward overcoming the constraints of current power conversion systems. Future efforts will focus on refining materials, improving circuit designs, and developing better packaging methods.

One challenge is that piezoelectric resonators physically vibrate, which means they cannot be attached to circuit boards using standard soldering techniques. New integration strategies will be needed to incorporate them into electronic systems, Mercier explained.

"Piezoelectric-based converters aren't quite ready to replace existing power converter technologies yet," Mercier added. "But they offer a trajectory for improvement. We need to continue to improve on multiple areas -- materials, circuits and packaging -- to make this technology ready for data center applications."

This project was supported in part by the Power Management Integration Center (PMIC), an Industry-University Cooperative Research Center (IUCRC) funded by the National Science Foundation (award number 2052809).

Materials provided by University of California - San Diego. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10958. Man buys two-bedroom apartment for just $1.70 in China’s Black Friday-style sale

A man in China has purchased an apartment for just $1.70 during a shopping promotion event, and it’s just as absurd as it sounds.

Shopping sales usually mean saving money on a shiny new TV or picking up a discounted game or two, but one lucky man in China took money-saving to an entirely new level by purchasing a two-bedroom apartment for just $1.70.

The property was sold through Chinese bidding platform Alibaba Auction as part of the country’s Double 11 event, an annual shopping festival that’s similar to Black Friday.

Located in Heilongjiang province in northeastern China, the seventh-floor apartment measures 68 square metres (roughly 732 square feet) and has two bedrooms.

According to the South China Morning Post, bidding started at one yuan (around $0.15) and was capped at 11 yuan (around $1.65). While 1,677 bidders took part, it was an anonymous shopper from eastern China’s Jiangsu-Zhejiang region who managed to secure the property.

Despite landing a new home for less than a cup of coffee, the buyer revealed he has no plans to relocate. Instead, he intends to use the two-bedroom property as a place to stay during winter holidays.

While the man may have purchased the apartment for $1.70, he will need to pay around 2,000 yuan (approximately $300) in deed taxes to complete the title transfer. SCMP also cited a warning from Beijing Jinsu Law Firm, which said buyers considering ultra-cheap properties should check for issues such as unclear ownership, unpaid taxes, and outstanding fees.

Either way, securing an apartment for just $1.70 puts it right next to owning an entire Japanese castle for $64,000 (yes, this is a real thing).

#10955. “Giant superatoms” could finally solve quantum computing’s biggest problem

Researchers at Chalmers University of Technology in Sweden have introduced a new theoretical design for quantum systems based on what they call "giant superatoms." This concept offers a fresh way to protect, control, and share quantum information, potentially bringing scientists closer to building large-scale quantum computers.

Quantum computers are expected to transform fields like drug discovery and encryption by solving problems that are far beyond the reach of conventional machines. However, progress has been limited by a major challenge known as decoherence. This occurs when quantum bits, or qubits, lose their information due to interactions with their surroundings. Even small amounts of electromagnetic noise can disrupt the fragile quantum states needed for computation.

"Quantum systems are extraordinarily powerful but also extremely fragile. The key to making them useful is learning how to control their interaction with the surrounding environment," says Lei Du, postdoctoral researcher in applied quantum technology at Chalmers.

Lei Du is the lead author of a study that outlines this new type of quantum system. The design is built around giant superatoms, which combine several important features. These systems reduce decoherence, remain stable, and consist of multiple interconnected "atoms" that function together as a single unit.

Giant superatoms bring together two previously separate ideas in quantum physics: giant atoms and superatoms. While each has been studied on its own, this is the first time they have been merged into a single system. These structures behave like atoms but are not found in nature. Instead, they are engineered by scientists (see fact box below).

The idea of giant atoms was first introduced by researchers at Chalmers over a decade ago and is now widely used in the field. A giant atom is typically designed as a qubit (which is the smallest unit of quantum information). Unlike ordinary atoms, it connects to light or sound waves at multiple, physically separated points. This allows it to interact with its environment in several places at once, helping it preserve quantum information.

"Waves that leave one connection point can travel through the environment and return to affect the atom at another point -- similar to hearing an echo of your own voice before you've finished speaking. This self-interaction leads to highly beneficial quantum effects, reduces decoherence and gives the system a form of memory of past interactions," explains Anton Frisk Kockum, Associate Professor of Applied Quantum Physics at Chalmers and co-author of the study.

Although giant atoms have improved understanding of quantum behavior, they have had limitations when it comes to entanglement. Entanglement allows multiple qubits to share a single quantum state and act as one coordinated system, which is essential for powerful quantum computers.

To overcome this limitation, the research team combined giant atoms with the concept of superatoms. A superatom consists of several natural atoms that share the same quantum state and behave collectively as one larger atom.

This combination is expected to make it easier to create complex quantum states needed for quantum communication, networks, and highly sensitive measurement systems.

"A giant superatom may be envisaged as multiple giant atoms working together as a single entity, exhibiting a non-local interaction between light and matter. This enables quantum information from multiple qubits to be stored and controlled within one unit, without the need for increasingly complex surrounding circuitry," explains Lei Du.

"Giant superatoms open the door to entirely new capabilities, giving us a powerful new toolbox. They allow us to control quantum information and create entanglement in ways that were previously extremely difficult, or even impossible," says Janine Splettstoesser, Professor of Applied Quantum Physics at Chalmers and co-author of the study.

Toward Scalable and Practical Quantum Systems

This work creates new possibilities for building quantum systems that are both scalable and reliable. The researchers plan to move from theory toward actually constructing these systems. Their design could also be integrated with other quantum technologies, serving as a building block for connecting different types of quantum platforms.

"There is currently strong interest in hybrid approaches, in which different quantum systems work together, because each has its own strengths," says Anton Frisk Kockum. "Our research shows that smart design can reduce the need for increasingly complex hardware and giant superatoms are bringing us one step closer to practically applicable quantum technology."

More on: Methods for protecting, controlling and distributing quantum information

The study shows that the way giant superatoms interact with light depends on their internal quantum states. This discovery gives researchers greater control over how quantum information moves through a system. They describe two different ways of connecting these structures to achieve useful outcomes.

In one setup, several giant superatoms are closely linked in a specific arrangement. This allows them to pass quantum states between each other without decoherence, meaning no information is lost.

In another setup, the atoms are spaced farther apart but connected in a carefully tuned way so that waves remain synchronized. This makes it possible to direct quantum signals and distribute entanglement over long distances.

Superatoms and giant atoms are engineered systems that behave like atoms rather than naturally occurring ones.

A superatom is a quantum system made up of multiple natural atoms that share a single quantum state and respond to light as one entity.

A giant atom, on the other hand, connects to light or sound waves at several separate points in space. It is called "giant" because it is larger than the wavelength of the light it interacts with.

Giant atoms have defined energy levels and follow the rules of quantum mechanics, yet they can reach sizes of up to millimeters, making them visible to the naked eye. Through electromagnetic or acoustic waves, they can interact with their surroundings at multiple locations at the same time. One way to picture this is as a single atom linked to a wave at several distant points. This unusual setup allows the atom to be influenced by the waves it produces.

Materials provided by Chalmers University of Technology. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10953. Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigns amid spy controversy

Nvidia CEO and co-founder Jensen Huang has resigned after 33 years leading the company, with an interim replacement taking over immediately.

The announcement on November 12 comes just four days after Nvidia launched an independent investigation into reports that Huang had been targeted by a foreign intelligence operation involving a suspected Chinese spy.

Huang, who co-founded Nvidia in 1993, has stepped down as president, CEO, and a member of the board of directors.

Nvidia confirmed that Colette Kress, its executive vice president and chief financial officer, will serve as interim CEO while the board searches for a permanent replacement.

Kress joined Nvidia in September 2013 and has overseen its financial operations during a period of significant growth. She will retain her existing responsibilities while serving as interim CEO, with the company considering both internal and external candidates for the permanent position.

The leadership change follows reports that Huang had an extramarital relationship with Alice Huang, a former executive at Singapore-based technology company Megaspeed, whom US intelligence officials reportedly suspected of working for Chinese intelligence.

The two are not related, and the allegations prompted Nvidia’s board to announce an independent investigation on November 8, overseen by outside legal counsel.

At the time, Nvidia said it had no reason to believe that national security or company secrets had been compromised. The company also expressed confidence in Huang’s leadership and confirmed that he would cooperate with the investigation.

Huang subsequently acknowledged being unfaithful to his wife in a message to employees, apologizing to his family and colleagues while maintaining his commitment to the company.

The controversy also attracted attention from US lawmakers, with three House committee chairs requesting a closed-door briefing with Huang on November 18 to discuss his interactions with Alice Huang and Nvidia’s security procedures.

China’s Foreign Ministry has rejected the espionage allegations, while Singaporean authorities have said they did not identify Alice Huang as a Chinese intelligence asset.

Shortly after the investigation, it was reported that Huang had a second affair with an executive from the company, and on November 12, Reuters reported a third alleged affair with a second employee.

Nvidia’s November 12 announcement did not address the status of its investigation or whether Huang would continue cooperating following his departure.

Alongside Kress’s appointment, Nvidia announced that Harvey C. Jones would become the company’s first-ever chair of the board.

Jones, who has more than 30 years of experience in the technology industry, said the company expects its operations and product roadmap to continue without interruption.

“Colette has helped lead NVIDIA through a period of extraordinary growth and has a deep understanding of our business, customers, operations and financial position,” Jones said.

“She is supported by an experienced leadership team that has been instrumental in executing NVIDIA’s strategy and advancing its technology roadmap. The Board has full confidence in Colette and the leadership team, and we expect the company’s operations, customer commitments and product roadmap to continue without interruption.”

Kress also addressed her appointment, thanking Huang for his contributions to Nvidia over the past three decades.

“NVIDIA’s strength has always come from its people, and together with our leadership team, I am committed to building on the extraordinary foundation Jensen created over the last 33 years,” she said.

“We will continue advancing the work that has made NVIDIA a leader in accelerated computing and AI, and execute on the opportunities ahead. Our focus remains on serving our customers, partners, developers and shareholders.”

Huang’s departure comes as Nvidia faces increased scrutiny over its leadership and business dealings with China, with its advanced AI chips remaining subject to US export restrictions.

#10949. This simple change stops robot swarms from getting stuck

Imagine a swarm of robots rushing to complete an urgent job, such as cleaning up an oil spill or assembling complex machinery. At first, adding more robots speeds things up. But after a certain point, the space becomes crowded, robots start interfering with one another, and overall progress slows.

This raises a simple but important question: in a limited area, how many robots can you deploy before efficiency starts to drop? Researchers at Harvard believe they have found a clear answer.

A new study from the lab of L. Mahadevan, the Lola England de Valpine Professor of Applied Mathematics, Organismic and Evolutionary Biology, and Physics, shows that adding a controlled amount of randomness to how robots move can reduce congestion and improve performance in crowded environments.

The work combines mathematical modeling, computer simulations, and real-world experiments. It demonstrates how basic local movement rules can lead to organized, efficient outcomes on a larger scale. The findings could influence how robotic fleets are designed and may even apply to human crowd management and traffic flow. The research was published in Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences and led by applied mathematics Ph.D. student Lucy Liu, with guidance from SEAS Senior Research Fellow Justin Werfel.

Why Randomness Helps Predict Complex Behavior

Studying dense crowds is difficult because individuals can take countless possible paths and interact in unpredictable ways, Liu explained. To simplify the problem, the researchers treated each robot as a basic unit with a small, adjustable amount of variation in its movement.

"This might be counterintuitive, because how could randomness make things easier to work with?" said Liu. "But in this case, when you have a lot of randomness, it becomes possible to take averages -- average distances, average times, average behaviors. This makes it a lot easier to make predictions."

To explore this idea, the team created computer simulations of robot groups, referred to as agents. Each agent started at a random location and was assigned a random destination. Once it reached its target, it immediately received a new one, mimicking continuous task assignment in real-world systems.

Each agent moved toward its goal with a tunable amount of variation, described as "noise." With no noise, agents moved in straight lines. With high noise, their paths became erratic and inefficient. However, this wandering also helped them navigate around one another.

The simulations revealed a clear pattern. When agents moved in perfectly straight paths, they quickly formed dense clusters and traffic jams that halted progress. When movement became too random, congestion disappeared but efficiency dropped due to excessive wandering.

Between these extremes, the researchers identified a sweet spot. In this range, agents occasionally bumped into one another and formed short-lived clusters, but still managed to slip past and keep moving. This balance allowed the system to maintain a steady flow.

Using these insights, the team developed formulas to estimate "goal attainment rate," or how many destinations are reached over time. These equations made it possible to determine the ideal combination of crowd density and movement randomness to maximize performance.

To confirm their findings, Liu collaborated with physicist Federico Toschi at Eindhoven University of Technology in the Netherlands. Together, they set up experiments with small wheeled robots in a lab equipped with an overhead camera.

Each robot carried a QR code so its position could be tracked and updated with new destinations. Although the physical robots moved more slowly and less precisely than the simulated agents, they displayed the same overall patterns.

The experiments supported a key idea: highly complex coordination does not require advanced intelligence or centralized control. Instead, simple local rules can produce effective group behavior, at least within certain density limits.

"Understanding how active matter, whether it is a swarm of ants, a herd of animals, or a group of robots, become functional and execute tasks in crowded environments using the principles of self-organization, is relevant to many questions in behavioral ecology," Mahadevan said. "Our study suggests strategies that might well be much broader than the instantiation we have focused on."

Liu noted that she has long been interested in designing safer and more efficient crowded spaces. This research points toward a future where the movement of large groups, whether robots, vehicles, or people, could be predicted and optimized using mathematical tools.

The results suggest that introducing controlled variability into movement patterns may improve flow in many real-world systems, from factory floors to city streets.

Funding for the research came from the National Science Foundation Graduate Research Fellowship Program under Grant No. DGE 2140743, along with grants from the Simons Foundation and the Henri Seydoux Fund.

Materials provided by Harvard John A. Paulson School of Engineering and Applied Sciences. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10947. The Hidden Problems Behind Website-to-CRM Integrations (and How to Prevent Them)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Connecting a website form to a customer relationship management (CRM) system may seem straightforward. A developer collects information from a form, sends it to an API endpoint, and checks whether a new contact appears in the CRM.

The integration may work perfectly during the first test. However, problems can appear after the website goes live. Some leads may not reach the CRM, duplicate contacts may appear, or important customer information may be missing.

These issues are not always easy to notice. A visitor might see a confirmation message even when the CRM has failed to save their information.

This guide explains common problems in website-to-CRM integrations and practical ways developers can prevent them.

A website-to-CRM integration allows information collected through a website to move into a CRM system. This usually happens through an API or webhook.

For example, when someone completes a contact form, the website collects their name, email address, phone number, and other details. The integration then sends this information to the CRM, where it can be stored and used by the relevant team.

An API integration provides a structured way for two applications to exchange information. Developers who need additional background can also read OpenHerald's overview of API integration.

The process usually involves several steps:

The website validates the submitted information.

The application sends a request to the CRM.

The CRM checks the request and processes the data.

The website or backend handles the response.

Each step needs to work correctly. If one part fails, the information may not reach its destination.

One important rule is to avoid displaying a successful submission message before the application has safely accepted and stored the data.

Data issues are among the most frequent problems in website-to-CRM integrations. They may not appear during development because test submissions usually contain simple, predictable information.

Real visitors enter data in different formats and may submit the same form more than once.

A visitor might complete a contact form and later download a resource using the same email address. If the integration creates a new CRM record every time, the same person may appear several times.

To prevent this, developers should decide how existing contacts will be identified.

Email addresses are commonly used for matching. Before comparing them, the application should remove unnecessary spaces and convert the address to a consistent format.

These values should be treated consistently.

Where supported, an upsert operation can create a new contact or update an existing one instead of creating unnecessary duplicates.

Website forms and CRM systems do not always use the same field structure.

A website may collect a visitor's full name in one field, while the CRM expects separate first-name and last-name values. Other fields may have different names or requirements.

Developers should create a clear mapping between website fields and CRM fields before building the integration.

They should also check which fields are required. If the CRM requires information that the website does not collect, the request may fail.

Different systems may store information in different formats.

For example, one application might use United Kingdom, while another expects UK. Dates, phone numbers, and country values can also create problems.

A reliable integration should validate and standardise these values before sending them to the CRM.

A contact record is more useful when the sales or marketing team knows where the visitor came from.

However, integrations sometimes send only basic contact information and leave out details such as campaign source, landing page, and referral information.

Developers should identify which tracking fields are useful and include them in the integration where appropriate.

Even an integration that works correctly at launch can stop working later.

Authentication credentials may expire, permissions may change, or an API provider may limit the number of requests an application can make.

Three areas deserve particular attention.

Many APIs use access tokens or other credentials to verify requests. Some tokens expire and must be refreshed.

If an application does not handle token renewal correctly, requests may begin failing without an obvious problem on the website.

Developers should implement the authentication process recommended by the CRM provider and monitor failed requests.

An API provider may restrict the number of requests allowed within a particular period.

A marketing campaign can suddenly increase website traffic and generate more form submissions than usual. If the integration exceeds the provider's limit, some requests may be rejected.

The application should recognise rate-limit responses and retry requests according to the provider's instructions.

An integration should not receive more access than it needs.

For example, a form submission service may only need permission to create or update contacts. Giving it full administrative access increases unnecessary security risks.

Use the minimum permissions required for the integration to function.

External services can experience outages, network delays, and temporary errors. If the website sends a form submission directly to the CRM without storing it first, a failed request could mean a lost lead.

A safer approach is to separate receiving a submission from delivering it to the CRM.

Validate the form information on the server.

Store the submission safely in your own system.

Send it to the CRM through a background process.

Retry temporary failures after an appropriate delay.

Flag permanent failures for manual review.

This approach gives the application another opportunity to deliver information when the CRM becomes available again.

A request might reach the CRM successfully, but the response could be lost because of a network problem. The website may then retry the same request, creating a duplicate contact.

Idempotency helps prevent this situation. The application can use a stable identifier for each submission so that repeating the same operation does not create another record.

For example, a combination of the form ID and a normalised email address may help identify repeated submissions. The exact approach depends on the CRM's API capabilities.

This is an illustrative example. Functions such as crmUpsertContact() and scheduleRetry() need to be implemented according to the application and CRM being used.

The important point is that every submission should have a known status: successfully delivered, waiting for another attempt, or requiring manual attention.

A working integration should also protect the quality and security of the information it collects.

Public forms can receive automated submissions from bots. Without appropriate protection, these entries can fill the CRM with unwanted records.

Validation should happen on the server, even if the website already checks information in the browser.

Some forms collect permission to send marketing communications.

If a visitor selects a consent checkbox, that information should also be passed to the CRM. Otherwise, the CRM may contain the contact details without a reliable record of the visitor's choice.

Where relevant, store the consent value, the wording or version displayed, and the time the visitor gave consent.

An integration should only send the information needed for its intended purpose.

Collecting unnecessary personal details increases the amount of information the application must protect and maintain.

Developers should also understand where submitted information is stored, including databases, queues, application logs, and the CRM.

Testing an integration before launch is important, but monitoring it afterwards is just as necessary.

A successful test does not prove that every future submission will work.

The following checks can help identify problems early.

Compare the number of website form submissions with the number of contacts successfully created or updated in the CRM.

A growing difference between the two numbers may indicate that some requests are failing.

Application logs should record the reason a request failed and include a submission identifier where possible.

Avoid storing complete personal details in logs unless there is a specific, justified need.

Developers should configure notifications when the integration's failure rate exceeds an acceptable threshold.

This makes it easier to investigate problems before they affect a large number of submissions.

A scheduled test submission can help confirm that the complete process is still working.

For example, a test lead can be sent periodically and checked to confirm that it reaches the CRM correctly.

Someone should be responsible for monitoring the integration.

This person or team should review errors, check API changes, monitor credentials, and investigate changes to CRM fields.

Without a clear owner, small problems can remain unnoticed for a long time.

Documentation makes integrations easier to maintain and troubleshoot.

It does not need to be complicated. A short reference document covering the main rules can save considerable time when another developer needs to work on the system.

Before deployment, developers should also test unusual situations.

Testing these situations helps reveal weaknesses that ordinary form submissions may not expose.

What is the most common website-to-CRM integration problem?

Duplicate contacts and incorrect field mapping are common problems. They usually happen when the website and CRM handle data differently.

How can developers prevent duplicate contacts?

Normalise email addresses, use a consistent matching rule, and use an upsert operation or idempotency mechanism where the CRM supports it.

Should a website send form data directly to a CRM?

For more reliable handling, the application should first validate and safely store the submission. A background process can then deliver it to the CRM and retry temporary failures.

How can I tell if a CRM integration has stopped working?

Compare website submission counts with CRM records, monitor failed requests, and schedule regular test submissions.

Who should be responsible for maintaining the integration?

Assign a developer or technical team to monitor the connection, review errors, maintain credentials, and respond to API changes.

Developers can make these integrations more reliable by validating information on the server, storing submissions before delivery, using safe retry mechanisms, and monitoring the results after launch.

The goal is simple: every valid submission should reach the CRM, and every failed submission should be visible and recoverable rather than silently lost.
