#10869. SpaceX wants to build AI data centers in space. Will it work?

Imagine if one company could become the railroad, electric utility and cloud-computing provider of the emerging space economy. That potential fueled excitement around the long-anticipated initial public offering of SpaceX. Investors are not simply betting on rockets anymore. They are betting on an entire orbital ecosystem.

Among the most ambitious and challenging ideas riding this wave of enthusiasm is something that sounds almost like science fiction: orbital data centers. SpaceX may be one of the most well-known companies seeking to build them, but it is not the only one.

The logic is seductive: Launch the data centers into orbit, where solar energy is abundant and land, water and local power grids are no longer constraints. As artificial intelligence drives an explosion in computing demand, companies are pitching orbital data centers as a way to escape the growing environmental and infrastructure pressures of Earth-based computing. Data centers often also face backlash from the public at having these centers located in their communities.

But there is a vast difference between launching satellites and operating an industrial-scale computing infrastructure in orbit. Space is unforgiving. Radiation damages electronics. The electronics generate enormous amounts of heat, and getting rid of that heat is surprisingly difficult in space. Repairs are extraordinarily expensive, and every pound launched into orbit still carries a significant cost.

We are engineering professors who study data center design and space systems engineering. Building a space-based data center will involve considerations from both sides.

First off, consider what goes into an Earth-based data center, like those that you’ve probably begun to see pop up everywhere. These facilities power cloud computing, video streaming, online banking, scientific computing and, increasingly, artificial intelligence. But a data center is much more than a room full of servers.

A data center needs several things to operate reliably. The first is electric power. Servers, networking equipment and storage devices consume large amounts of electricity, and that power demand is growing rapidly with AI.

The second is cooling. Almost all the electricity consumed by servers eventually becomes heat. If that heat is not removed quickly and reliably, equipment performance drops, failures increase and the data center can shut down. Cooling systems often include air handling units, chillers, cooling towers, pumps and, increasingly, liquid-cooling equipment. In many facilities, cooling is the largest energy consumer after the computing equipment itself.

The third is physical infrastructure, including the necessary land, buildings, structural support, backup power, water systems, communication networks and maintenance access. Data centers also need to be close enough to users and network backbones to provide fast digital services.

In short, Earth-based data centers are large electrical and thermal infrastructure systems built around computing hardware.

So what would it take to build these data centers in space, and why are companies finding this possibility such an interesting business proposition?

As on Earth, these data centers would require massive amounts of power. In space, this power would come from solar panels. The Sun always shines in space and can’t be blocked by clouds. However, depending on the orbit the solar panels are put in, the Earth may shadow them for some portion of the orbit.

And even the best solar cells available today can convert only about half the sunlight that hits them to electricity.

Another potential advantage found in space is cooling. The cold background of space (near minus 455 degrees Fahrenheit, or minus 270 degrees Celsius) creates an opportunity: waste heat from the data center could escape into space through radiators, keeping the electronics cool.

In principle, that design could eliminate some of the bulky and water-intensive cooling infrastructure used on Earth. However, those thermal radiators would require a large amount of surface area, and that would be in addition to the area required by the solar panels.

In space, there is no air to blow across hot equipment and help heat escape. The heat has to leave as infrared radiation, which is a relatively slow process. As a result, removing 10 megawatts of waste heat can require radiator surfaces comparable to the size of two football fields.

Space-based data centers could also avoid some of the local conflicts that come with building large data centers on the ground. Many communities resist new data center developments because of their land use, energy and water demand, and noise and environmental impact.

A space-based system would avoid competing for local land and water resources, and it would not generate neighborhood noise or require local zoning approval in the same way.

However, space is already getting crowded, and launching thousands of large orbital data centers would accelerate this issue. Orbital debris and micrometeorites are hazards because they can puncture the space data center, and a worst-case collision could destroy it and create even more space debris.

The frequency of space launches necessary to send all the equipment to orbit may also become a concern for some communities. SpaceX has had protests at its launch complex in Boca Chica, Texas, from local activists who argue that its rocket testing and launches damage the surrounding environment.

All that data would need to be sent between Earth and these data centers – and between the data centers themselves – using radio waves or laser communications systems. Although satellite constellations such as Starlink and Amazon Leo have demonstrated that doing this is possible, the amount of data sent to and from space would balloon.

These data centers, along with their solar panels and radiators, cannot be launched in one piece and would need to be assembled in space. This process would require new equipment for in-space servicing, assembly and manufacturing.

Another key challenge is the refresh cycle of computing hardware. Data center servers are not built to last forever. Operators on Earth usually replace or upgrade hardware every three to five years as chips improve, workloads change and equipment ages.

And equipment failures can require replacing components. The refresh and repair processes are relatively straightforward on Earth, where workers can physically remove and replace servers.

In space, refresh and repair becomes much harder. Hardware sent to orbit may be difficult or too expensive to upgrade. If the computing platform cannot be updated, or too many components fail, it may become obsolete long before the surrounding infrastructure reaches the end of its useful life.

In a field where performance improves so rapidly and demand from computing continues to increase, this hurdle could prove a major economic and operational challenge.

Then there is the harshness of space. These data centers would be in a near vacuum, with constant radiation hitting them. And depending on their orbit, they would go from hot when in the sunlight to cold in Earth’s shadow many times a day. All of these challenges, and more, are issues that will need to be addressed.

Despite these challenges, companies are moving forward with designing space-based data centers. SpaceX just announced the design for its AI1 Compute Satellite, which it hopes to use as an orbital data center spacecraft. However, this satellite is 100 to 1,000 times less capable than current Earth-based data centers.

Not every computing task makes sense to do in space. Many data center applications depend on fast response times and close connections to users on Earth. Financial transactions, interactive AI services and most cloud applications are extremely sensitive to delay.

More feasible early applications may be those that are less latency-sensitive and more tightly connected to space operations. Examples could include processing Earth observation data from satellites, military or intelligence data processing, scientific computing related to space missions, or specialized computing for satellites and other space assets.

In other words, the first viable space data centers may serve space-based customers before they compete with mainstream cloud data centers on Earth.

Materials provided by The Conversation. Original written by Sven BilÃ©n and Wangda Zuo. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10868. How Product Teams Can Use AI User Research to Test Assumptions Before Sprint Planning

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Sprint planning is on Thursday, and the top of the backlog rests on three things nobody has checked. Users want the feature, they will find it, and they will understand what it does when they do. Each belief is plausible, and each has already been written into a ticket.

AI user research uses language models to simulate or analyze user conversations, so a team can put a question to a modeled audience in minutes instead of recruiting people for weeks. Used well, it gives a product team a cheap first check on backlog assumptions. Used badly, it produces confident-sounding agreement. This guide covers a two-day routine that gets the benefit and avoids the trap.

An assumption is worth testing when the plan depends on it and a wrong answer would change the ticket. "Users like clean interfaces" fails that test. "Analysts re-apply the same filters every session" passes, because if it is false the saved-filters feature shrinks or disappears.

The timing matters because planning leaves little room for research. The Scrum Guide says Sprint Planning is timeboxed to a maximum of eight hours for a one-month Sprint, and the first question the team answers is why the Sprint is valuable. Checks run before that meeting inform the answer. Checks run during it eat the time meant for deciding.

Write each risky item as a short card that names the belief, the consequence of being wrong, and the decision it feeds. A card forces the team to say what would change its mind, which most tickets never do. Here is an example:

Aim for three to five cards. A team that tries to test the whole backlog will test none of it, and the cards on the riskiest items give the best return.

AI user research comes in two forms. The first analyzes data you already hold: clustering support tickets and summarizing interview transcripts. It works on real evidence, so its main risk is a sloppy summary.

The second form interviews simulated people. AI user research of this kind builds a panel of modeled personas matched to your target profile, asks them your questions, and reports the themes. It needs no recruiting and returns results quickly, which fits a two-day window. Its output comes from a model of users and not from users, and that distinction should appear in every planning document that cites it.

A simulated audience helps most when you want to sharpen a question, and least when you need proof. Nielsen Norman Group's 2024 review of synthetic users found them useful for generating hypotheses and preparing interview guides. It warned against using them for final decisions or concept validation, and for niche populations, because AI tends to please and "every idea is often seen as a good one."

Spend Tuesday collecting answers and Wednesday reading them skeptically. On Tuesday, put each card's question to the modeled audience in neutral wording, and ask what would make them not use the feature. A question phrased as "Would saved filters help you?" invites a yes, while "Walk me through setting up your weekly report" does not.

On Wednesday, compare the answers with what you already know from support tickets and analytics. Mark each assumption as supported, contradicted, or unclear. Treat a unanimous answer with suspicion, because real audiences rarely agree on anything. Also be wary of lists in which the modeled users "seem to care about everything," which is how Nielsen Norman Group describes their weak prioritization.

Whatever stays unclear or high risk goes to real people. Jakob Nielsen's classic analysis found that five users find about 85% of usability problems, assuming comparable users, and he recommended several small rounds over one large one. An AI check is a good way to decide what those five people should be asked.

Each mark maps to a different decision. A contradicted assumption means the ticket is cut or reduced to its smallest useful version. A supported assumption that rests only on simulated evidence is not yet safe, so schedule a real-user check as the first task in the sprint, ahead of the build work that depends on it. An unclear assumption should not receive full scope.

The cost of skipping this step is real. A 2019 analysis of 615 product subscriptions by a product analytics vendor found that about 12% of features generate 80% of daily usage volume, and 80% of features are rarely or never used. That is one vendor's customer base, and the figures cover features that customers chose to track, so treat them as a rough guide. They still suggest that most of a backlog is a bet.

AI user research cannot replace talking to real users. Nielsen Norman Group found that synthetic users gave overly optimistic accounts of their own behavior, such as claiming to finish every course in a study, and that they cannot supply real past experience. It also called presenting synthetic findings as real-user findings "an unethical way to share what you've learned."

Two habits keep a team honest. Label every piece of evidence in the planning document as modeled, observed, or reported, and require that any feature built on modeled evidence has a real-user check scheduled. The review also warned that teams starting with synthetic research may get comfortable and skip the real kind, so put the real check on the calendar before the first AI result arrives.

Can AI user research replace interviews with real users? No. It is a cheap first pass that helps you write better questions and spot weak assumptions. Anything that drives a significant build still needs real people.

How many assumptions should a team test before planning? Three to five. Pick the ones where a wrong answer would cut or reshape a ticket.

How do I stop simulated users from just agreeing with me? Use neutral, open questions, ask what would make them avoid the feature, and treat unanimous answers as a warning sign.

What should I do with results I cannot verify? Mark them as modeled, give them less weight than observed evidence, and schedule a real-user check before committing full scope.

Sources: Scrum Guide, Sprint Planning; Jakob Nielsen, "Why You Only Need to Test with 5 Users" (March 18, 2000); Nielsen Norman Group, "Synthetic Users: If, When, and How to Use AI-Generated 'Research'" (June 21, 2024); 2019 Feature Adoption Report (615 product subscriptions, vendor-reported).

#10867. AMD shares how it can retain 95% of server performance with just half the DRAM, sidestepping memory shortages with MEXT

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

MEXT offloads cold memory pages to flash storage and serves them up with a predictive AI model.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

In June, AMD overtook the predictive memory engine known as MEXT, which claims to offer upwards of 99% of the performance of a system with full DRAM capacity while running with as little as 25% of the physical DIMMs. The technology offloads cold memory pages to flash storage and uses an AI model to serve pages before they’re needed, and AMD says it plans to extend the tech across a wide swath of its IT infrastructure next year.

The pitch behind MEXT seems too good to be true. According to AMD and MEXT founder Gary Smerdon, the technology can cut the physical DRAM required in a server by 20% to 75% depending on the application and performance target. That missing memory still registers as installed DRAM to the OS. Instead of DRAM, however, MEXT uses relatively inexpensive flash storage to offload cold pages, flying them back in as they’re needed with an AI model. In some of AMD’s internal workloads, such as Synopsys PrimeTime, the company says it’s able to achieve 95% of the performance of a full server while running at 50% DRAM capacity with MEXT.

AMD has deployed MEXT internally on 145 servers, which it says will expand to 2,000 servers next year. In addition, the company has announced a partnership with HPE to bring MEXT to customers. Currently, we’re in phase 1 of the rollout, with some EPYC Venice servers carrying a “MEXT Ready” badge and a dedicated NVMe drive or bay for the offload. Phase 2 brings MEXT licenses out to these servers, as well as the RAM View application. AMD hasn’t said when Phase 2 will begin.

The AI-driven DRAM pricing surge impacts more than just gaming PCs, with enterprises also feeling the brunt of price increases, even if they aren’t running AI workloads. If MEXT is able to achieve what AMD claims, it could be transformative given the current market. Because of that, it’s worth understanding what exactly MEXT is, as well as what AMD claims it can do.

Although MEXT is exciting in concept, it’s obvious that AMD is taking a conservative approach to the rollout. Extraordinary claims require extraordinary evidence, after all. The applications of MEXT are currently narrow, though AMD says it’s expanding the workloads it’s using MEXT on with promising early results.

The basic setup of MEXT is simple in concept: cold pages go to flash, AI brings those pages back as needed, and it’s as if the memory was never missing in the first place. The main problem should be obvious. Flash is orders of magnitude slower than DRAM, so streaming pages back into memory from flash as they’re needed isn’t feasible. MEXT’s trick is using an AI model to predict the pages you’ll need. And, as long as the model is able to stay ahead by at least the transfer latency from the SSD, it’s as if the physical DRAM was installed the whole time.

This isn’t a crazy concept considering how a transformer-based LLM, which MEXT uses, operates. If you’re unfamiliar with the inner workings, this open-source visualization breaks down the concept of a transformer AI model cleanly. The foundational concept is next token prediction, which underpins the design of modern LLMs. Given a set of tokens, the LLM determines which token (word, portion of a word, etc.) is most likely to come next. It makes a prediction. In the case of MEXT, it’s predicting what page is needed next, not what word comes next.

Memory offloading to flash isn’t a new concept, but MEXT founder Gary Smerdon says that AI is the critical difference in this technology. MEXT itself is deployed as a Linux kernel module that works with any Linux distro. It uses an “ensemble of AI models,” with the primary transformer-based LLM driving the technology. And critically, it doesn’t come pre-trained.

Instead, AMD says that MEXT trains in under a second when first deployed, operates on one EPYC core without a GPU, and continually trains as the workload progresses. This approach makes sense given the varied workloads that MEXT may apply to. The idea is that it would learn the access patterns of the application in order to drive that prediction engine.

MEXT isn’t perfect, but it doesn’t need to be perfect in every situation. For instance, the order of stored pages isn’t important. With text, an LLM needs to predict the correct text in the correct order, but with MEXT, the order doesn’t matter. As long as the page is in DRAM when it’s needed, that’s fine.

In order to understand the application of MEXT to different workloads, AMD has developed RAM View to go alongside the tech. It’s a memory utilization tool that shows which pages are hot and which have gone cold. As you can see from the example above, a system with 1.5 TB of memory shows near-full utilization across the full workload, but around half of the pages stored in memory haven’t been touched in five minutes or more at any given time.

AMD provided this generalized example and didn’t clarify what workload it was testing. That’s very important, as the utilization breakdown will look vastly different depending on the application(s) running on the server. Initially, AMD is applying MEXT to critical EDA applications it uses internally, such as Synopsys PrimeTime, but the company says it has focused on optimizing agentic AI, database, and media and entertainment workloads as well.

This is important not only because memory access patterns change between applications, but also because performance in those applications reacts differently when a system doesn’t have enough memory. The “memory wall,” as AMD calls it, should be a familiar concept regardless of whether you’ve ever touched a server. With MEXT, AMD says it’s trying to extend this memory wall, which may look different for different applications.

For instance, the company is confident that many workloads can reduce capacity by around 20% to 25%. As the reduction increases, however, the applications become more specialized, and the performance delta becomes greater.

AMD has shared some internal MEXT results, which look impressive, though as usual, it’s always smart to handle first-party benchmarks like this with skepticism. Starting with PrimeTime, where AMD has focused much of its attention on MEXT up to this point, it says servers running at 50% capacity retain 95% of the performance of a full server, and going down to as little as 25% capacity still maintains 80% of the application’s performance.

PrimeTime demands a lot of memory for large designs. There are some applications where performance is nearly identical, according to AMD. In a 3-node cluster running in Azure with 5th-gen EPYC CPUs, AMD says MEXT offers 99% of the performance of the full cluster while running at 50% DRAM capacity with the Spark SQL workload from the TPC-DS benchmark. SQL databases try to cache as much data as possible in memory to prevent I/O bottlenecks, creating a golden opportunity for tech like MEXT that wants to free cold pages.

Perhaps the most applicable workload at the current moment, however, is agentic AI, and AMD says MEXT has similar applications there. Across a five-agent concurrent geomean, AMD claims 99% of the performance of a full server with 50% memory capacity, and 85% of the performance with 25% capacity. With a “memory-hungry” agent, AMD claims 90% and 88% performance for 50% and 25% capacity, respectively.

The slide you can see above has footnote tags. However, AMD has not shared the footnotes with us yet, so we don’t have a view into how these tests were run. Naturally, we assume AMD has, at minimum, represented the performance of MEXT in the best light possible. We can’t do a full analysis without knowing how the tests were run, however.

What is interesting is AMD’s cautious approach to rolling out MEXT. It’s an EPYC-exclusive feature right now, though AMD left the door open for support on future products (consumer devices, such as Strix Halo) far down the road. It will be sold as a license, bundled with the RAM View software, but we don’t have a timeline on when that will happen. And, despite a partnership with HPE to push out “MEXT Ready” servers, we still don’t know when MEXT itself will be ready for deployment beyond AMD’s walls.

Given that conservative approach, there are likely pitfalls to MEXT that we’re not seeing yet, and it’s hard to imagine it will be broadly applicable to every server, at least at the 50% and 25% DRAM capacity levels that AMD showed off with its benchmarks.

However, even being able to trim 25% of a server’s DRAM capacity represents a massive cost savings in the current supply-constrained environment. If some workloads can push that to 50% capacity, that’s even better. Of course, we’ll need to see how MEXT holds up once it’s available broadly, but if AMD’s claims hold water, it could become a defining technology in data centers that can’t seem to get enough memory.

Jake Roach is the Senior CPU Analyst at Tom’s Hardware, writing reviews, news, and features about the latest consumer and workstation processors.

#10864. Millions of exploding stars could soon reveal dark energy's secrets

Researchers led by the Institute of Cosmos Sciences of the University of Barcelona (ICCUB) have developed a new technique that could greatly improve how scientists study the expansion of the Universe and investigate the mysterious force known as dark energy.

Published in Nature Astronomy, the research introduces a framework called CIGaRS that can extract far more information from Type Ia supernovae, the powerful stellar explosions used to measure vast cosmic distances. Unlike many current approaches, the method relies mainly on imaging data rather than expensive spectroscopic observations. The advance is expected to help astronomers take full advantage of the enormous datasets that will soon arrive from next generation sky surveys, particularly those conducted by the Vera C. Rubin Observatory.

Type Ia supernovae occur when white dwarf stars explode. Because these explosions reach nearly the same intrinsic brightness, astronomers use them as "standard candles": by comparing their actual brightness with how bright they appear from Earth, researchers can calculate their distance.

These measurements played a crucial role in the discovery that the Universe is expanding at an accelerating rate. Scientists attribute that acceleration to dark energy, one of the most significant unsolved questions in modern physics.

However, there is an important complication. Type Ia supernovae are not perfectly identical.

How Host Galaxies Affect Supernova Measurements

Over the past 20 years, astronomers have found that a supernova's observed brightness is influenced by the galaxy where it occurs. Supernovae found in older or more massive galaxies can appear slightly different from those occurring in younger or less massive galaxies.

Researchers have typically accounted for these differences using relatively simple correction methods. While useful, these approximations can limit the accuracy of distance measurements and, in turn, the precision of cosmological studies.

A Unified Model of Supernovae and the Universe

The new framework addresses this challenge by modeling multiple factors simultaneously. Rather than treating each component independently, the researchers built a single, integrated model that includes the supernova explosions themselves, their host galaxies, the dust that alters their light, changes in supernova rates throughout cosmic history, and even the expansion of the Universe.

By connecting all of these ingredients within one statistical and physical framework, the team can capture relationships that are often overlooked when the pieces are analyzed separately.

"A powerful way of modelling the Universe is to simulate it ab initio in the computer using Bayesian inference," says Raúl Jiménez (ICREA-ICCUB), co-author of the study. "This provides a way to vary all possible parameters at the same time to predict what Universe we live in. Furthermore, by having this capacity, one can look into possible 'unknown unknown' systematics to understand their effect. The impact of these systematics in our inference is arguably the most important missing ingredient in current approaches to model the Universe."

Using Artificial Intelligence To Analyze the Cosmos

Building such a comprehensive model would normally require enormous computing power. To make the approach practical, the researchers turned to a modern technique called simulation-based inference.

The process begins with scientists generating large numbers of simulated universes based on physical models. A neural network (a type of artificial intelligence) then learns how the simulated observations relate to the physical properties that produced them. Once trained, the system can compare real astronomical observations with its simulations and determine the most likely underlying parameters.

This strategy makes it possible to analyze tens of thousands of supernovae simultaneously, a task that would be impractical using traditional techniques.

Accurate Galaxy Distances From Images Alone

One of the study's most significant findings is that the framework can determine galaxy distances (redshifts) with high accuracy using only imaging data.

Redshift measures how much a galaxy's light has been stretched as the Universe expands. It provides information about both the galaxy's distance and how far back in time we are observing it.

According to the researchers, the new method delivers redshift estimates with precision comparable to spectroscopic measurements, but without requiring spectra. This capability is especially important because upcoming surveys are expected to identify millions of supernova candidates, while only a small percentage can realistically receive spectroscopic follow-up observations.

Ready for the Rubin Observatory Data Deluge

The Vera C. Rubin Observatory, currently being built in Chile, is expected to begin a decade long survey of the sky in the near future. During that mission, it will discover an unprecedented number of supernovae. Roughly 99% of those objects will be observed only photometrically, meaning through images taken in different colors rather than detailed spectra.

The CIGaRS framework was specifically developed with this challenge in mind.

"Unlike other frameworks, which require analytic simplifications, our no-compromise end-to-end simulation-based inference approach is uniquely capable of extracting the full cosmological and astrophysical information from the Rubin Observatory's hard-earned data, while avoiding the pitfalls of selection and modelling biases," says Konstantin Karchev (ICCUB-SISSA Trieste), lead author of the study.

The benefits extend beyond measuring dark energy. The framework also provides new information about the origins of Type Ia supernovae themselves.

By reconstructing how supernova occurrence rates vary with the ages of stars in different galaxies, the model helps scientists investigate long standing questions about the systems that eventually produce these explosions.

The researchers found that combining physics based simulations with artificial intelligence can overcome several limitations of current cosmological methods. They estimate that the approach could improve cosmological constraints by as much as a factor of four compared with traditional techniques that depend only on a relatively small sample of spectroscopically observed supernovae.

As the Rubin Observatory prepares to usher in a new era of astronomical discovery, tools such as CIGaRS could help scientists extract the maximum amount of information from its observations and gain a deeper understanding of the Universe.

Materials provided by University of Barcelona. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10862. Microsoft outage brings fax machines back into action across Japanese offices

Ongoing Microsoft outages have led companies across Japan to dust off fax machines, with employees now using the old-school tech to complete tasks during the widespread blackout.

A widespread Microsoft 365 outage has disrupted email and workplace services around the globe, leaving many without access to the everyday tools they need for work.

While some services have started to recover, local governments and firms across Japan have turned to a familiar sight in offices during the 1980s and 1990s: fax machines.

These chunky devices are used to send copies of documents over telephone lines, allowing staff to communicate and confirm transaction details while email remains unavailable.

So, despite the tech being outdated compared to more modern devices, it turns out that sometimes relying on old-school equipment can be the perfect modern solution.

On November 13, companies across Japan began using fax and other alternatives to keep communications moving. Disruption has also reached government offices, with some asking people to contact them by telephone or fax while their email systems remain unavailable.

Since then, Japanese users across social media have been praising fax machines and other analogue tech for enabling them to continue communicating throughout the outages.

“Unbelievable, faxes are saving the day yet again…it reminded me of when faxes were getting renewed attention after the recent cyberattack. When you’re in a bind, you can always count on analog,” wrote one user on X.

“Our company wasn’t affected by the Outlook issue, but since other companies were, emails were only going one way to our phones. We used to exchange photos by texting them to each other, but in the end, even in this day and age, fax was just unbeatable,” added another.

Japanese office workers complain about men wearing shorts over “leg hair harassment”

Others have also praised the use of analogue tech, particularly as a means to keep connected with one another during times of disaster.

“We need to keep analog around. Because it’s so convenient, and it comes in handy in situations like this,” wrote one commenter. “It’s the same as when people can’t use their cell phones during a disaster and flock to public phones.”

#10861. Linux kernel reviewer steps down from memory management duties due to influx of AI slop patches

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

"Nowadays it's pretty much half of what I see, if not more."

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Linux Kernel is probably one of the best ecosystems to observe regarding the impact of AI in programming. The critical importance of its code and its maintainers' quality standards mean that patches, including but not only AI slop, go through rigorous review and are often discarded. Still, the amount of AI-produced code is often far higher than what the human reviewers can deal with. Lorenzo Stoakes, a lead Linux reviewer for memory management, has elected to step down from some of his former duties for exactly that reason.

Stoakes announced his decision today in a thread on the Linux kernel mailing list (LKML). He'll be leaving the core, reclaim, Rust, THP (Huge Pages), and miscellaneous sections of the kernel memory management section, while staying on for his remaining duties, including KVM. The partial departure appears amicable, and the developer doesn't appear to be angry or bitter. He says that his decision was due to the mounting pressure on his inbox, that he'll wrap up the patch series he's currently working on, and he'll occasionally pitch in regardless.

However, the underlying cause is also clear: "Of course the AI wave has been horrible, and is getting significantly worse. At first it was once in a while that I'd see slop. Then it was at least once a week. Then every few days. Nowadays it's pretty much half of what I see, if not more."

This is a sentiment echoed by many programmers in the open-source community, and the latest in a running series of AI-provoked project departures, submission rules adjustments, and strategic shifts. AI-assisted programming has arguably been both a boon and a curse for open-source software. The project's open nature means that anyone can find a bug and report it or fix it, or add functionality — all of which are good things.

However, that also means that literally anyone can contribute, and in a world ridden with low-effort AI slop and untold amounts of AI agents, that's... suboptimal. Open-source projects are arguably the hardest-hit because they very rarely have the adequate manpower or funds to keep the code up to date, much less deal with the output of a million robotic monkeys on a million digital typewriters.

Linus Torvalds, creator and headmaster of Linux, has taken a pro-AI stance, supported not only by the speed of additional contributions, but also the fact that bots often don't make mistakes that even seasoned developers do. This topic has been the subject of a lot of LKML discussion, and the current stance is that AI-assisted code commits don't need to be marked as such.

The problem, then, has arguably become one of filtering: it's easy enough to be accepting of AI contributions when your day isn't spent waddling through mud to find the one gold nugget, which is what Stoakes was seemingly weary of.

Back in July, he'd already brought up concerns about the "recent wave of slop in [memory management]," adding that "the amount of time wasted on AI slop is already too much and we're only at the start of this." Even as recently as a month ago, he said that "what kills the project is having 1,000 slop submissions that 2 or 3 people have to do something with."

#10858. Former Call of Duty producer says older games were “more fun” with less SBMM

Former Call of Duty producer Mark Rubin has criticized skill-based matchmaking, saying it makes casual matches feel repetitive and leaves players with little sense of how much they’ve improved.

Rubin spent a decade at Infinity Ward, where he was a producer on Call of Duty 4: Modern Warfare and the original Modern Warfare 2, before serving as executive producer on Modern Warfare 3 and Ghosts. He then had the same role on Ubisoft’s XDefiant.

That shooter famously removed skill-based matchmaking, or SBMM, to a minimum, meaning ability wasn’t considered when creating lobbies. This is something that’s been a long-running complaint for Call of Duty fans, and it’s carried on into Modern Warfare 4.

Now, Rubin has doubled down on his criticism of the feature.

In a post on X, he argued that constantly facing opponents of a similar ability makes matches feel repetitive, while removing the need to improve.

“SBMM means you keep playing the same game against the same people (ie people who play the same way and the skill as you even if they are different players),” he explained. “So the games lack diversity especially when the constant challenge keeps players playing the meta instead of diversifying their loadouts.

“You also never feel yourself get better because the games are always the same difficulty. As you get better so does everyone in your lobbies.”

My 2 cents: SBMM means you keep playing the same game against the same people (ie people who play the same way and the skill as you even if they are different players) so the games lack diversity especially when the constant challenge keeps players playing the meta instead of…

He went on to acknowledge that SBMM is ideal for Ranked Play, but argued that public matches should be more varied.

“Generally there will be some people better than you and some worse,” he added. “And for me that is what made older CoDs more fun because SBMM was minimal.”

Modern Warfare 4 boss responds to SBMM backlash as players call it “miserable”

Modern Warfare fans upset as evidence suggests skill-based matchmaking

Ex-CoD dev suggests SBMM explanation from Activision contained “bullsh*t” claim

In a follow-up post, Rubin explained how he’d make tougher matchups feel more rewarding for less skilled players.

“Something I wanted to do is reward players differently based on skill. If you’re lower skilled and you kill a better player you should get rewarded for that.”

This comes as Infinity Ward faces backlash over the SBMM matchmaking in Modern Warfare 4, but there’s currently no word on whether it will be addressed in a future update.

#10857. AI-Powered Pentesting for DevSecOps: 7 Checks Developers Should Make First

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Security testing used to happen once or twice a year, usually after a release had already shipped. That rhythm does not fit teams that deploy daily. AI-assisted tools now promise testing that runs continuously, chains steps together on its own, and writes up what it found.

For developers, the interesting question is not whether these tools are impressive. It is what has to be true in your pipeline before you let an automated attacker loose on your own systems. This article covers seven checks to make first, each with a pass condition, and one short code example you can run. Some checks, such as written authorization and cost tracking, usually belong to a security lead or manager. For those, the developer's job is to confirm they exist, not to own them.

Testing systems you do not have permission to test is not a gray area. Make sure someone with authority has approved the targets and the time window, and keep that approval with your records. Run the first tests against staging with test data. Production comes later, once you know how the tool behaves and what load it creates.

Pass: The approval is on file, and the SOC knows the window and source IPs.

An automated tester should never decide for itself what it is allowed to touch. Define scope in a file that your pipeline can check: which hosts are in bounds, when testing may run, and whether anything destructive is allowed.

SitePoint's article on security patterns for autonomous agents uses an open-source AI penetration-testing agent as its case study. It covers sandboxed execution, tool permissions scoped to each role, approval gates for high-risk actions, and audit logging. Scope enforcement belongs in that same family of controls. Here is a minimal check that refuses any target outside the allowlist or time window:

Call this before every scan job, and log the result. A tool that is out of bounds should fail loudly instead of quietly continuing.

Pass: An out-of-scope target fails the job, and egress outside the allowlist is blocked.

Findings are only useful if they reach the people who can fix them. Most teams already have a place where security results show up in pull requests or a security tab. SitePoint's walkthrough on automating DevSecOps static analysis with GitHub Actions shows how results written in the SARIF format appear as inline annotations and can block a merge when severity crosses a threshold. If your pentest tool can export SARIF or a similar format, send its findings to the same place.

Pass: The tool exports SARIF or similar, and results show up in PRs or the security tab.

An AI tool can produce a long list of findings. To judge whether they are complete and sensible, you need a reference for what good web security testing covers. The OWASP Web Security Testing Guide is a free, widely used checklist of test categories for web applications. It covers web applications only, so APIs and network services exposed to the internet need their own coverage. Map the tool's findings against it, and note which categories it never touched.

Pass: You know which OWASP WSTG categories you expect the tool to cover.

Vendors describe AI pentesting in similar terms: agents plan attack steps, try them, and verify what works. For a view of how a security vendor explains the approach, CyCognito's overview of AI penetration testing walks through a workflow from scoping to autonomous testing, validation and reporting, and notes that human review stays part of the process. All vendor claims need testing in a demo against your own systems before you rely on them.

Ask the same questions of any tool you evaluate: What does it test beyond known checks? How does it confirm a finding is real? What does it send to external services?

Pass: The vendor has clear answers, including where the target list comes from.

Automated findings need review. A person should confirm that a reported path is exploitable, judge its business impact, and decide the fix order. Make sure this is set up before the first run. Without it, a team can end up with a long backlog of unverified findings and little trust in the tool.

Pass: There is a named reviewer and time set aside each cycle.

Running agents continuously can add up, especially when they call large models. Make sure you can track what each run costs and how many real issues it finds, so you can compare it with other approaches. You also need to be able to rerun the specific test that found a problem after a fix ships and keep the result. A finding is only closed when the retest passes.

Pass: Both are possible before you commit to running the tool continuously.

Does AI pentesting replace manual penetration testing?

Not entirely. Automated testing can run more often and cover more ground, but people are still needed for scoping, complex attack paths and judging impact.

In staging, with test data, inside a written scope and time window.

What should I do with findings from the tool?

Send them to the same place as your other security results, have the named reviewer check them, and retest after fixes.

AI-assisted pentesting can give development teams faster and more frequent security feedback, but only when it runs inside clear limits. Before the first run, check that targets and the window are approved, scope is enforced by your pipeline, findings will land where developers work, you have a baseline to judge the output, you know what the tool tests and sends out, someone owns the review, and you can measure cost and retest. The scope check above is small, but it makes sure the tool stays inside the lines you set.

#10856. Quantum mechanics once baffled scientists. Now it's changing the world

For much of the early 20th century, quantum mechanics was one of the most puzzling ideas in science. The theory challenged conventional thinking and left even leading physicists struggling to make sense of its implications. A century later, it has become the foundation of technologies that influence daily life, including lasers, microchips, secure communications, and emerging quantum computers.

In a new perspective article published in Science, Dr. Marlan Scully of Texas A&M University reflects on the remarkable evolution of quantum mechanics, from an abstract theory about tiny particles to a powerful framework helping researchers tackle some of the most difficult questions in science.

"Quantum mechanics started as a way to explain the behavior of tiny particles," said Scully, who is also affiliated with Princeton University. "Now it's driving innovations that were unimaginable just a generation ago."

Scully has played a major role in advancing the field. He co-authored the influential textbook Quantum Optics, a resource that has educated generations of physicists. His research in coherent nanoscale laser spectroscopy helped make it possible to study molecules with atomic-scale precision. He has also developed groundbreaking concepts involving quantum heat engines, which challenge traditional assumptions about thermodynamic efficiency and may one day lead to new energy technologies.

From Schrödinger's Cat to Quantum Technology

One of the most famous illustrations of quantum mechanics came in 1935, when Erwin Schrödinger proposed his cat paradox. The thought experiment suggested that a cat could exist in a state that is both alive and dead until it is observed. Schrödinger introduced the idea to highlight how strange quantum theory appeared.

Today, those once-bizarre concepts have moved far beyond philosophical debates.

"That 'quantum weirdness' is no longer just a philosophical puzzle," said Scully. "It's the foundation of quantum computing, quantum cryptography and even gravitational wave detection."

The foundations of quantum mechanics were built by pioneers including Schrödinger and Werner Heisenberg. They developed two different mathematical approaches, wave mechanics and matrix mechanics, to describe quantum systems. Over time, these approaches were unified and contributed to the development of quantum field theory, which explains how particles interact through electromagnetic and nuclear forces.

Their work expanded on Niels Bohr's early atomic model, which pictured electrons orbiting the nucleus much like planets orbit the sun. While later discoveries refined that picture, Bohr's model helped pave the way for modern quantum theory.

Among the most important concepts in quantum mechanics is quantum coherence. This phenomenon allows particles such as atoms and photons to remain linked in a coordinated state, even across significant distances.

Quantum coherence led directly to the development of the laser, a technology that many once believed could never work. Today, lasers are used throughout modern society, from supermarket barcode scanners to vision correction procedures and advanced scientific instruments.

Coherence is also closely tied to quantum entanglement, the phenomenon that prompted Albert Einstein to describe it as "spooky action at a distance."

Entanglement enables particles to share information through uniquely quantum properties. These effects form the basis of quantum encryption systems and improve the sensitivity of sophisticated instruments such as the Laser Interferometer Gravitational-Wave Observatory (LIGO), which detects tiny ripples in spacetime.

Quantum Heat Engines Challenge Classical Limits

One of the more surprising applications of quantum physics involves quantum heat engines.

Traditional engines are constrained by the Carnot Limit, which defines the maximum efficiency allowed by classical thermodynamics. Researchers have found that by exploiting quantum coherence, it may be possible to create engines that exceed those classical limits.

"It's a striking example of how quantum principles can rewrite the rules of classical physics," Scully said.

The influence of quantum mechanics now extends far beyond physics.

In biology, techniques such as coherent Raman spectroscopy allow researchers to examine viruses and other structures at the nanoscale, providing valuable insights into the microscopic world.

Quantum ideas are also shaping efforts to understand the universe itself. Scientists working on concepts such as string theory and quantum gravity are attempting to reconcile quantum mechanics with Einstein's theory of relativity, one of the biggest unsolved problems in modern physics.

Researchers are even applying quantum concepts to the long-standing challenge of understanding turbulence. The chaotic motion of air and fluids affects weather patterns, climate systems, and aircraft performance. By studying superfluid helium, a substance that displays unusual quantum behavior, scientists are uncovering patterns that could improve climate modeling, storm prediction, and aviation safety.

Despite a century of success, quantum mechanics continues to raise profound questions.

Can gravity be quantized (i.e., does gravity behave like other forces at the quantum level)? Could quantum computers transform medicine and materials science? What new insights about the universe might emerge from future quantum technologies?

Scully believes the search for answers is only beginning.

"At the start of the 20th century, many thought physics was complete," he said. "Now, in the 21st century, we know the adventure is just beginning."

Five Ways Quantum Mechanics Affects Everyday Life

Materials provided by Texas A&M University. Original written by Lesley Henton. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

Intel and AMD NPU support is planned, but founder says neither will be used for language models.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A team led by Cyanogen co-founder Kirt McMaster has unveiled Kora, a new Linux desktop OS with integrated AI models and agents that can split workloads across CPU, GPU, and NPU resources, as well as other machines on the same local network. We reached out to the firm for more info.

Kora, the first product from Kora Systems Inc., previously Playtron, carries the tagline “The Operating System for Intelligence” and has its own Cortex “hybrid inference engine” technology, which is not open source and is optimized for local models. The OS, built “from the kernel to the shell,” runs local models by default and cloud models when needed, and is expected to arrive on PCs in 2027.

The desktop follows the AI-focused motif with “neural workspaces” and a “neural file system,” which refer to sandboxed workspaces and files indexed by semantic meaning. Kora has three operating modes: “Work," "Create," and "Play." That last base is covered by GameFrame, which descends from Playtron’s GameOS. Humain OS, which Kora is developing with Saudi AI company Humain, is the enterprise variant of this OS.

Currently, the only NPU named in the release material is the Hexagon NPU found in the Snapdragon X2 Elite, which Qualcomm pegs at 80 TOPS. On the X2 Elite, Cortex can schedule AI workloads across the CPU, GPU, and NPU, as well as other systems on the local network, which could be a novel capability if it works beyond the Qualcomm hardware.

McMaster told Tom’s Hardware that the Snapdragon X2 Elite NPU runs Google's Gemma 4 26B model at 25 tokens per second for decode. Intel and AMD NPU support is in the works, but according to the developer, neither NPU will be used for LLMs, as GPUs are “just better for LLM workloads on x64.”

Each NPU vendor has its own software stack, or runtime, and prefers that models are compiled in its own format: Qualcomm with QAIRT/QNN, Intel with OpenVINO, and AMD with Ryzen AI software. The press kit doesn't identify support for any Intel or AMD chip, NPU runtime, model format, quantization, or speed figures for those platforms.

Our earlier Windows testing showed the same kind of divide: Snapdragon reached its NPU through the QNN provider under ONNX Runtime, while Intel’s work ran through IPEX, its extension for PyTorch, on the GPU because it was faster than the NPU.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

When asked about cross-vendor support, McMaster reiterated that “only Hexagon on Snapdragon X2 Elite is supported today.” Intel NPU support is planned, with “conversations underway,” though McMaster later clarified that neither Intel’s nor AMD’s NPU will be used for LLMs. On AMD’s Ryzen AI chips, Kora will likely use the NPU for embedding and speech-recognition models through ONNX.As for what drives the Qualcomm Hexagon NPU, McMaster said Kora uses the “llama.cpp Hexagon backend” and “the upstream FastRPC driver.” That suggests the operating system reaches the NPU through the open-source llama.cpp project, not Qualcomm’s own QAIRT/QNN runtime, by way of the FastRPC driver in the mainline kernel.

Language models; KORA cites Gemma 4 26B at 25 tokens per second

Likely embedding and speech-recognition models; not language models

When asked about model formats, McMaster said, “we predominantly support GGUF,” llama.cpp’s model file format, and that Lemma, the inference engine inside Cortex, “can split model layers across processors as needed.” Lemma is built on llama.cpp, whisper.cpp, stable-diffusion.cpp, and ONNX. McMaster says that which runtime is used depends on the model's requirements, and more runtimes will be added down the line.

For today, llama.cpp has a Hexagon backend for Snapdragon and an OpenVINO backend for Intel CPUs, GPUs, and NPUs, although the latter is still marked “In Progress,” and no AMD NPU backend, which would explain why AMD goes through ONNX. AMD NPUs can run LLMs on Linux through the separate Lemonade 10.0 stack with FastFlowLM, as reported by Phoronix in March, though that isn’t the route Kora has chosen.

Beyond NPU stacks, those hoping to run Kora will need to be mindful of their system's available memory. The Gemma 4 26B model Kora uses, which Google lists as Gemma 4 26B A4B, needs about 14.4GB of memory to load its weights at 4-bit (Q4_0) quantization, by Google’s estimate, before any context window, while third-party listings put the download for the Q4_K_M file Kora uses at about 16–17GB. The model only activates about 4 billion of its 26 billion parameters per token but still needs to be fully loaded into memory.

McMaster said that with the model, the X2 Elite reaches “25 TPS decode throughput and 550 TPS prefill throughput,” or tokens per second, running “entirely on the Hexagon NPU with 32K context.” A dense model, rather than a mixture-of-experts, would be far slower. Not given are the RAM configuration or the specific X2 Elite chip.

On memory, McMaster said that “we recommend 64GB because it gives you access to better models,” but that is not a firm requirement. The company projects that “about 70 percent of the model calls it manages will run locally in 2027.”

Kora says its enterprise-focused Humain OS is due first and will be “commercially available in 2027,” on OEM PCs using the Snapdragon X2 Elite. Kora will follow "later in 2027.”

McMaster says that “Kora will be posted on our site and downloadable for anyone to install.” Included will be a list of recommended configurations, “but it should be installable on most modern PCs.” Exact pricing and the overall business model will be shared closer to the date of availability.Edit: 11/11/2026 9:06am PT: Added further follow-up details provided by the maker.
